\paragraph{Where in the target the error sits.} With the per-event predictions saved
(\code{evaluation.save\_predictions}), the squared residual of the arithmetic arm at its best
HPs was binned in deciles of each process's own standardized target
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/residual\_vs\_target.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:residual_vs_target}). For
the bulk the error is spread over the target (median share $0.13$ in the lowest decile and
$0.16$ in the highest, against $0.10$ for flat), so the range of the target is not, for
most of the catalog, one end being unlearned. Two groups are, and they are the two
struggler classes. The signed one-loop pools put $65$--$75\%$ of their MSE in the lowest
decile ($u\bar d\to t\bar bg$, $ud\to ud\gamma$, $uu\to uu\gamma$ at one loop): the region
around the sign change, where the signed log is linear and the standardized target is near
zero, is where the error lives, and the transform chosen for this class flattens exactly
that region. The resonant and $t$-channel $2\to2$ pools put $50$--$86\%$ in the highest
decile ($ee\to u\bar u$ and $ee\to d\bar d$ at one loop $80$--$86\%$; $u\bar u\to ZZ$,
$u\bar u\to Z\gamma$, $ee\to ZZ$, $ee\to Z\gamma$, $u\bar u\to WW$ at one loop $50$--$70\%$):
the pole, or the forward peak of a $t$-channel exchange, which the $s$-channel off-shellness
input does not describe. So ``better preprocessing'' is two concrete moves, one per
class: a target-side subtraction of the known propagator factors, $s$- and $t$-channel,
for the resonant pools (the target's version of the input that removed the LO needle,
built from the same sidecars), and a transform with resolution near zero for the signed
class (or a separate sign prediction). Each is a $500$-step test at this working point with
three repeats per arm.

\paragraph{Back to $1000$ steps, with repeats: the signed-log scale is not the lever.}
The working point returns to $1000$ steps on the $5$k pools (with the evaluation overhead
gone a run is $17$ minutes), the arithmetic mean at the sweep's best HPs, and every arm
runs three init seeds (\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/seed\_arms.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:seed_arms_slq}).
The seed spread at this horizon is about $20\%$ on the combined number ($0.0286\pm0.0060$
for the baseline) and $10$--$30\%$ per class. The first test is the scale of the signed log
for the sign-changing pools (\code{data.signedlog\_quantile}), since the residual profile put
their error at the sign change where the transform is linear below the scale: at the $10^{-3}$
and $10^{-4}$ quantiles instead of $10^{-2}$ the combined number is unchanged ($0.0283$,
$0.0290$), every other class is inside its band, and the signed class itself goes the wrong
way, median $0.112\pm0.030$, $0.123\pm0.013$, $0.147\pm0.012$. Enlarging the log-like region
does not help and shrinking the linear one hurts, so the error near zero is the sign
structure, not the flat region; a separate sign prediction (a classification head next to
$\log|x|$) is the remaining move for this class, and it is a model change.

\paragraph{The propagator subtraction on the target works.} The second move takes the
known pole out of the target: every event's $|\mathcal M|^2$ is multiplied by
$\prod_k\big((s_k-M_k^2)^2+M_k^2\Gamma_k^2\big)/M_k^4$ over the process's massive propagators
(\code{data.target\_propagators}; $Z$, $W$, $t$, $H$ with the MadGraph widths; the scanned
mass where a scan sets it), built from the same sidecar masks as the off-shellness input,
so the network learns the smooth remainder and the factor is divided back out at
evaluation. Only propagators whose $s_k$ straddles $M^2$ on the train pool carry a factor:
a $t$-channel exchange or a sub-system pole out of reach never does, and on a first
attempt with every massive propagator the factor doubled the median range of the target
instead of removing a peak. With the rule, $178$ of $478$ processes carry one ($376$ of
$1657$ candidate propagators). Three seeds at $1000$ steps against the baseline's three
(Fig.~\ref{fig:seed_arms_tprop}): the combined loss goes from $0.0286\pm0.0060$ to
$0.0223\pm0.0024$, $378$ processes improve and $100$ worsen (geometric mean of the ratio
$0.78$), and the class the move was aimed at halves, resonant $2\to2$ from $0.0227$ to
$0.0106$ at the median and from $0.058$ to $0.018$ at the $90$th percentile. The one-loop
resonant pools gain most ($ee\to u\bar u$, $d\bar d$, $b\bar b$ at one loop $5$--$9\times$;
$u\bar d\to e\nu$ and its one-loop twin $14$--$17\times$; the $M_Z$-scan variants $2$--$10\times$),
every other class moves down inside or at the edge of its band, and nothing is worse
outside it. The residual profile with the subtraction on
(Fig.~\ref{fig:residual_vs_target_tprop}) has $23$ processes left with over a third of their
error in the highest decile, against $45$ before, and they are now the $t$-channel pools
($u\bar u\to ZZ$, $WW$, $Z\gamma$, $u\bar d\to WZ$, $ee\to ZZ$, $ee\to WW$ and their one-loop
versions): the massless exchange the straddle rule leaves alone, and the next factor to
build (power one for a fermion exchange, two for a boson, since $|\mathcal M|^2$ goes like
$1/t$ and $1/t^2$ respectively). The signed class moves from $0.112$ to $0.097$, inside its
band; its sign structure stays the open move.

\paragraph{The $t$-channel factor: a targeted gain on the $2\to2$ pools, and an instability.}
The massless $t$-channel exchange (\code{data.target\_propagator\_tchannel}) carries
$(|t|/\mathrm{median}|t|)^p$, $p=1$ for a fermion and $2$ for a boson, with $|t|$ floored at
the pool's $10^{-3}$ quantile, and only for the exchange between one initial and one final
leg: the first rule, every massless spacelike internal line, put factors on $2370$ lines and
took the target's median range from $13$ to $29$; the one-initial-one-final rule keeps $964$
on $429$ processes, $13$ to $17$. Applied to every process it does what it is for and costs
elsewhere (Fig.~\ref{fig:seed_arms_tch22}, three seeds): the $t$-channel $2\to2$ pools halve
($ee\to ZZ$ $0.0056\to0.0032$, $ee\to Z\gamma$ $0.0054\to0.0025$, Bhabha $0.019\to0.008$,
$u\bar u\to ZZ$ at one loop $0.0093\to0.0033$), tree $2\to2$ goes from $0.0031$ to $0.0021$
and positive one-loop from $0.016$ to $0.011$, but the $2\to4$ class loses outside its band
($0.050\pm0.003\to0.060\pm0.007$): on a six-particle amplitude the one-initial-one-final
exchange is not the dominant structure and its factor only adds range. Restricted to the
$2\to2$ processes (\code{target\_propagator\_tchannel\_max\_final: 2}) the $2\to2$ gain stays
($0.0022$) and the $2\to4$ class returns to its baseline ($0.053$); the combined number is the
$s$-channel arm's ($0.022$), on the two seeds that trained. That is the setting kept. The caveat
is an instability: two of the nine runs with the factor diverged (one under each rule, none of
the fifteen without). Counted at its best checkpoint (validation $4$ of $20$, combined $0.23$),
the $2\to2$-only arm's diverged seed puts the arm at $0.091\pm0.12$ over three seeds, its tree
$2\to2$ median at $0.037\pm0.061$; the rule was kept on the other two. Both diverged runs went
the same way, a normal descent to step $200$--$250$ and then a jump by five to ten at the
end of the $170$-step warm-up, at peak learning rate, never recovered; no non-finite
gradient in the second case. The factor widens the $2\to2$ targets and that tips the
arithmetic mean at this rate, which is the joint lr/aggregation question of the full-horizon pretraining sweep, not a
property of the factor itself. On a single process the factor is not a gain: fitted alone, the
$t/u$-channel $2\to2$ probes of Sec.~\ref{sec:ladder} are $11$--$290\times$ lower without it, and
only $ee\to W^+W^-$ (a single $\nu$ exchange) is better with it.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_d.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_e.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/seed_arms_tch22_f.pdf}}
\caption{$1000$ steps, $5$k pools, three seeds: the $t$-channel factor on every process and
on the $2\to2$ processes only (three seeds; the second rose after its best checkpoint at
validation $4$ of $20$ and enters there), on top of the massive propagators divided out of the
target; per-class ECDF of the per-process $\vlnr$ at the best checkpoint,
the band the min/max over seeds. (a) tree $2\to2$, (b) resonant $2\to2$, (c) tree $2\to3$, (d) tree $2\to4$, (e) positive one-loop, (f) signed one-loop.}
\label{fig:seed_arms_tch22}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The joint-training gap: a difference of exponents, plus a cross-class cost.}
With the target changes in, every class still sits above what its processes reach alone at
the same per-process compute. Two measurements say what that gap is. First, the steps curve on
the full pools with every horizon tuned: one single-fidelity DyHPO per horizon
(\code{sweeps/catalog\_steps\_t<N>\_mean}, $500$--$8000$ steps), then three seeds at each best on
the full pools under the arithmetic and the geometric mean (\code{runs/steps\_full\_t*},
\code{runs/steps\_fullgeo\_t*}; $1000$--$8000$ steps, both propagator factors, sign head), against
two reference processes per class trained alone on their full pools at $\mathrm{bs}=1024$, one
single-fidelity DyHPO of six trials per step count ($33$--$1072$ steps,
\code{sweeps/solob1k\_t<S>\_<process>}). Every value is at the run's best checkpoint. The axis is
per-process compute, $C_p$ = the events of process $p$ seen times the FLOPs per event at its
multiplicity: in the joint run the uniform sampler draws events in proportion to pool size, so a
tree process ($10^5$ train events) sees $38$ events per step and a one-loop process
($5\times10^4$) $19$, where the step grid of the references assumed $34$ for every process; on
$C_p$ both sides sit at the compute they actually had. Each curve is fitted with
Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling} (\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/steps\_tuned.py},
Figs.~\ref{fig:steps_tuned} and \ref{fig:steps_tuned_alpha}). The joint exponent is below both
references of its class in every class under the arithmetic mean ($\alpha=0.39$ for the resonant
$2\to2$ pools, $0.68$ for tree $2\to4$, $0.85$ for signed one-loop, $0.95$ for tree $2\to3$, $1.00$
for tree $2\to2$, $1.19$ for positive one-loop, against $1.13$--$2.09$ alone) and in every class
but positive one-loop under the geometric mean ($0.21$ to $1.52$; positive one-loop $1.81$ against
$1.65$ and $1.25$). The resonant $2\to2$ pools are the extreme: $0.39$ and $0.21$ against $1.69$
($ee\to u\bar u$) and $1.34$ ($ee\to d\bar d$) alone. The joint floors fit at zero in every class
(no curvature over $1000$--$8000$ steps), and the reduced $\chi^2$ is $50$--$290$ under the
arithmetic mean and $330$--$6900$ under the geometric one, so the class median is not described
within its seed spread by a single power law; the geometric-mean fits include the
$4000$-step seed whose loss rose $78$-fold after its best checkpoint (validation $8$ of $20$).
Per process, the joint exponent is below its alone value for $11$ of the $12$ references under the
arithmetic mean ($ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop equal, $1.25$) and for $9$ of $12$ under the geometric
mean (above: $ee\to u\bar u$, $ee\to d\bar d$, $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop). The joint exponent
falls with multiplicity as Prin.~\ref{prin:mult} has it (tree classes $1.00$, $0.95$, $0.68$ under
the arithmetic mean, $1.52$, $1.24$, $0.68$ under the geometric one); the references alone do not
(Fig.~\ref{fig:alpha_mult_steps}; $1.25$--$2.09$ at two final particles, $1.46$--$2.04$ at three,
$1.13$ and $1.72$ at four), which is what the paragraphs below take apart. Two
caveats on the references. Their HPO is thin: six trials per sweep, and DyHPO's fixed seed puts
the same three start-up configurations in every sweep, which are the best trial in $36$ of the
$72$ sweeps; the loss spreads by $0.27$ decades within a sweep, a third of it explained by the
learning rate and $6\%$ by the other hyperparameters together
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/solo\_b1k\_inspect.py}). And the two signed references were rerun
(\code{sweeps/solob1kv\_t<S>\_<process>}): until \code{6939d77} the single-process validation of a
signed pool included the sign head's cross-entropy, so its recorded validation, checkpoint and
HPO objective were $6$--$10$ times the MSE.
Second, at $1000$ steps each multiplicity trained alone at the same per-process compute
(\code{recipes/catalog\_v2\_train\_scan\_2to*.yaml}, batch $34\times$ the subset's size, the
rate not retuned, $5$k pools) beats its loss in the full catalog on nearly every process:
$2\to3$ alone at $0.56$ of its joint loss ($176$ of $176$), $2\to4$ at $0.74$ ($184$ of $185$),
$2\to2$ at $0.79$ ($98$ of $117$), each process at its run's best checkpoint
(Fig.~\ref{fig:joint_vs_solo}). Sharing one network across multiplicities costs $20$--$45\%$ at
this horizon, on top of the exponent effect.

\paragraph{The references at the canonical batch: the exponent does not fall with multiplicity.}
The $\mathrm{bs}=1024$ references cover only the start of training ($33$--$1072$ steps, at most
$1.1\times10^6$ events), so they were repeated at $\mathrm{bs}=16384$ over $63$--$4000$ steps
(\code{sweeps/solo16k\_t<T>\_<process>}, \code{sweep/gen\_solo16k\_configs.py}), everything else as
the $\mathrm{bs}=1024$ configs, which match the joint steps runs (diagram encoder off,
\code{mass\_from\_momenta} on, arithmetic mean). The search is reduced to the knobs that mattered
at $\mathrm{bs}=1024$ (the learning rate explained $34\%$ of the within-sweep variance, the warm-up
$3\%$, EMA $1\%$, $\lambda$ and $\eta_{\min}$ $0.2\%$ and $0.1\%$): $\mathrm{lr}$ within $\pm\frac12$
decade of $\mathrm{lr}^*(t,D)$ on the high-$D$ row of the measured surface, the warm-up in
$[0.05,0.2]$, $\eta_{\min}=0$, $\lambda=10^{-8}$, EMA off; six trials per step count, twelve at
$63$--$250$ steps. Doubling the trials at the low end moved the best value by at most a factor
$1.7$ and left half the cells unchanged. With the floor-aware law over the seven step counts
(Figs.~\ref{fig:solo16k} and \ref{fig:alpha_mult_16k}; the bracket is the range of leave-one-point-out
refits) the tree references give $2.27\,[2.20,2.33]$ and $1.27\,[1.23,1.35]$ at $2\to2$,
$1.78\,[1.73,1.85]$ and $1.76\,[1.71,1.96]$ at $2\to3$, $1.60\,[1.44,1.69]$ and $1.33\,[1.27,1.38]$ at
$2\to4$; the resonant $2\to2$ pools $2.01$ and $1.88$, positive one-loop $1.71$ and $0.98$, signed
one-loop $2.14$ and $1.23$. The joint exponents stay below the references in every class. What
multiplicity changes is where a curve levels off: at $4000$ steps the $2\to2$ pools are at
$2.5\times10^{-6}$--$2.7\times10^{-5}$, $2\to3$ at $5\times10^{-5}$--$9\times10^{-5}$, $2\to4$ at
$6.5\times10^{-4}$--$2.1\times10^{-3}$, and the two $2\to4$ processes and the signed loops fit
floors near $10^{-3}$.

\paragraph{Against the from-scratch set of Table~\ref{tab:scaling}: not the pool.}
Table~\ref{tab:scaling} measured the same four processes ($ee\to\gamma\gamma$, $u\bar u$, $u\bar ug$,
$u\bar ugg$) falling with multiplicity ($2.37$, $1.74$, $1.34$, $0.97$). That set differs from the
references in its pools (uniform $\sqrt s$ over $91$--$1000$\,GeV, $10$--$1000$ for $ee\to\gamma\gamma$,
$70$k train events, against the catalog's mixture-shaped pools over $25$--$1000$\,GeV, $100$k), its
target (no propagators divided out, no $t$-channel factor, no sign head), its inputs (the nine
properties only), its width ($4$ heads) and its search ($15$ trials, $\mathrm{lr}$ over
$4.5$ decades). Its exponents lean on a first point at $4$ steps, a practically untrained model
($0.6$--$1.0$): without it they are $2.61$, $2.90$, $2.16$, $1.37$, still falling with
multiplicity, the references much less (Fig.~\ref{fig:solo_old_new}). The pool was tested
directly. At the old set's best HPs per step count, the old pools under the catalog setup do not
reproduce its curves ($ee\to\gamma\gamma$ rises from $9\times10^{-5}$ at $1265$ steps to
$3.3\times10^{-4}$ at $4000$, $ee\to u\bar ug$ and $u\bar ugg$ stall at $7\times10^{-4}$ and
$1.8\times10^{-3}$; only $ee\to u\bar u$ matches), so the old HPs do not carry over to the new setup
(\code{sweep/run\_fixed\_hp.py}, \code{scripts/job\_fixed\_hp\_old.sh}). Swept like the references
(\code{sweeps/solo16kflat\_t<T>\_<process>}, the pool the one difference), the old pools give
$1.71\,[1.45,2.66]$, $1.94\,[1.56,2.59]$, $1.74\,[1.63,2.13]$, $1.50\,[1.36,1.87]$ against the
catalog pools' $2.27$, $2.01$, $1.78$, $1.60$, and the two sets of curves lie on each other. The
pool is not what flattens the multiplicity dependence. The one place the old set is far ahead is
$ee\to\gamma\gamma$: it reached $2.2\times10^{-7}$, where the catalog setup levels off at
$1.5\times10^{-5}$ on either pool, a floor set by something in the setup ($ee\to\gamma\gamma$
carries two $t$-channel factors, the sign head and the new inputs).

(grey; another target, so its level does not compare), the catalog references at $\mathrm{bs}=1024$
and $16384$, the old pools under the catalog setup at the old set's best HPs, and the old pools
swept like the references; each with its floor-aware fit
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/solo\_old\_new.py}).}

\end{figure}

\paragraph{Seed noise and the best-of-trials bias.}
Each reference point is one training run, the best of six to twelve trials. The five $2\to2$
references were rerun at every step count at their best trial's HPs with three more seeds
(\code{scripts/job\_seed\_2to2.sh}, $105$ runs; \code{analysis/catalog\_v2/solo16k\_seeds.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:solo16k_seeds}). At fixed HPs the loss of a cell spreads by a factor $1.1$--$30$
across seeds, most at the long horizons of the resonant pools: at $4000$ steps $ee\to d\bar d$
ends at $2.4\times10^{-6}$ and $6.1\times10^{-6}$ for two seeds and at $2.9\times10^{-4}$ and
$4.6\times10^{-4}$ for the other two. One run never trained: $ee\to\gamma\gamma$ at $1000$ steps,
seed $3$, at $0.42$--$0.45$ from step $450$ to the end (kept at its best checkpoint, $0.42$). The
exponent of a single seed's curve moves by up to $1.1$ ($ee\to\gamma\gamma$: $1.19$ to $2.27$); the
means over the four seeds are $1.76\pm0.42$ ($ee\to\gamma\gamma$), $1.43\pm0.12$ ($u\bar u\to u\bar
u$), $1.53\pm0.30$ ($ee\to u\bar u$), $1.41\pm0.42$ ($ee\to d\bar d$) and $1.10\pm0.09$ ($ee\to
b\bar b$ at one loop), so the spread of the $2\to2$ exponents seen in the sweeps ($0.98$--$2.27$) is
mostly run-to-run noise; $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop is the one clearly lower. The sweep's best
trial gives the steepest exponent in three of the five ($2.27$, $2.01$, $1.88$ against fresh-seed
means $1.59$, $1.37$, $1.25$): the long horizons scatter most, so taking the best of several trials
lowers them most and steepens the fit. Every exponent in this section from a best-of-trials
reference carries that bias and an uncertainty of $0.1$--$0.4$ that leave-one-point-out does not
capture; the joint exponents, fitted over three seeds, do not.

\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/solo16k_seeds_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/solo16k_seeds_d.pdf}} \\[1ex]

best trial (seed $42$, black), every run at its best checkpoint and none left out:
(a) $ee\to\gamma\gamma$, (b) $u\bar u\to u\bar u$, (c) $ee\to u\bar u$, (d) $ee\to d\bar d$,
(e) $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop; (f) the exponent fitted to each seed's curve and their mean and

\paragraph{Intrinsic dimension of the learned representation: the kinematic count.}
The intrinsic dimension of what a trained model represents was measured on the best checkpoint of
every reference cell (\code{tools/measure\_id.py}): the representation the output reads is the
event mean of the per-particle features entering the backbone's readout (the readout and the pool
are both linear), and its dimension on the first $1000$ test events is the TwoNN estimate of
\code{IntrinsicDimDeep}, averaged over three random $90\%$ subsets. The loaded models reproduce their
runs (test MSE over validation loss $1.00$ at the median, $0.84$--$1.19$ for $80\%$ of the cells).
The dimension is the number of independent kinematic invariants, $3n_{\rm fs}-4$ with $\sqrt s$
varying (Fig.~\ref{fig:id_solo}): $2.0$--$2.4$ for every $2\to2$ process, $5.0\pm0.6$
($u\bar d\to W^+ZZ$) to $7.1\pm0.6$ ($ee\to u\bar ug$) at $2\to3$ (count $5$), $8.3$--$9.6$ at
$2\to4$ (count $8$), the uncertainty the spread over step counts, larger than each measurement's
own resampling spread ($0.02$--$0.23$). It is set within the first hundred steps and is the same at
both batches and on the old pools. Against it, the $2\to2$ references sit near
$\alpha=4/\mathrm{ID}$, and the $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ ones far above it ($1.2$--$2.1$ at an ID of
$5$--$10$, where $4/\mathrm{ID}$ is $0.4$--$0.8$): the representation grows with multiplicity as
the phase space does, the exponent does not. The set of Table~\ref{tab:scaling} could not be
measured: its checkpoints predate the ninth particle property and do not load in the current model.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_v2/id_solo.pdf}
\caption{(a) Intrinsic dimension of the learned per-event representation per reference process,
grouped by multiplicity, for each set (bar: the spread over step counts); dashed, $3n_{\rm fs}-4$.
(b) The exponent (bar: leave-one-point-out range) against the intrinsic dimension; dashed,
$\alpha=4/\mathrm{ID}$ (\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/id\_solo.py}).}
\label{fig:id_solo}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The same curve under the geometric mean: the aggregation sets the $2\to2$ slope,
not the $2\to4$ one.} The horizon runs repeated at identical settings (lr $9.7\times10^{-3}$,
$\lambda=3.8\times10^{-8}$, warm-up $0.17$, no EMA, $5$k pools, both propagator factors, sign
head; three seeds per point) with only the training aggregation changed: the geometric mean,
and the geometric mean floored at $\tau=10^{-2}$ (\code{runs/steps\_geo\_t*},
\code{runs/steps\_tau1e-2\_t*}; \code{joint\_vs\_solo.py --arm}, \code{steps\_agg.py}),
Table~\ref{tab:steps_agg} and Fig.~\ref{fig:steps_agg}.
Under the geometric mean the gain per doubling spreads from $1.3$ to $3$--$6$ by class, where
under the arithmetic mean it sits between $1.2$ and $2.4$; tree $2\to2$ gains $3.5$ and $3.2$
per doubling against $2.4$ and $1.9$ and ends nine times lower ($4.1\times10^{-5}$ against
$3.6\times10^{-4}$), and positive one-loop gains $5.9$ and $3.9$ against $2.3$. But the class
that makes up most of the catalog does not move: tree $2\to4$ gains $1.5$--$1.8$ per doubling
under both means, and the geometric mean keeps it twice higher at every horizon. The resonant
$2\to2$ pools stall under both at $1.2$--$1.6$ per doubling, $5\times10^{-3}$ at $4000$ steps.
It is not the data: validation over train MSE is $1.00$ for the class at $4000$ steps
($1.06$--$1.12$ for the multi-particle classes). The trade is the familiar one at every horizon
(Fig.~\ref{fig:steps_agg_curves}): at $4000$ steps a combined median of $0.0097$ against
$0.0071$, and the signed class $3.3$ times higher. The floored arm's third seed at $4000$ steps
rose after its best checkpoint and never recovered (Fig.~\ref{fig:steps_agg_curves}: best at
validation $4$ of $20$, step $800$, then the combined loss jumps from $0.057$ to $1.8$ between
steps $1200$ and $1400$ with finite gradients, non-finite ones follow from about step $1900$; it
ends $34$ times above its best; none of the eighteen runs under either mean did), the same
signature as the $t$-channel runs and the second instability at this rate. At its best
checkpoint that seed is at the loss of an $800$-step run, so the floored arm's $4000$-step
column (Table~\ref{tab:steps_agg}) is above its $2000$-step one in every class; over its other
two seeds it sits between the two means. A per-process weight driven by each process's exponent
deficit would redistribute the gradient between the $2\to2$ and $2\to4$ classes, which these
arms already show is a trade, and would leave the two things none of them moves: the $2\to4$
slope and the resonant stall.

\paragraph{The sign head: a real but modest gain on the signed class.} With
\code{training.sign\_head} the sign-changing pools take $\log|x|$ as their target
(\code{abslog:<s>}, floored at the signed-log scale) and the network's second output
channel is a sign logit trained per event by a binary cross-entropy on those pools only
(weight one); validation stays the MSE on the magnitude target with the sign accuracy logged,
and the raw-space metrics apply the predicted sign. Three seeds on top of the $s$-channel
subtraction: the sign is learned, $98\%$ accuracy at the median over the $48$ pools and
$88$--$89\%$ on the worst ($u\bar u\to d\bar dg$ at one loop), against $98.7\%$ and $86\%$ for
the sign of the inverted signed-log prediction. The magnitude has to be compared on a
common footing since the two arms have different targets
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/signed\_compare.py}: the absolute error of $\log|x|$ over the
$95\%$ of events above the scale): the median over pools goes from $0.38$ to $0.32$ and the
per-pool $90$th percentile of events from $1.16$ to $0.94$, a $15$--$20\%$ gain; every other
class stays inside its band ($0.0237\pm0.0021$ combined against $0.0223\pm0.0024$). The
sign structure was the right diagnosis but not the large lever: the signed class remains
the worst by a factor of five over the next one, so its remaining error is in the
magnitude of a target that spans tens of decades on either side of zero, and the
per-class read-out stays the same with the sign taken out.

\section{Structure transfer: one process to one process, and the ladder}\label{sec:ladder}
% ==========================================================================

The catalog pretraining (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}) is set aside for a study built to tell
one story with data-scaling curves. The question every part answers: if the pretraining gains a
structure $S$, does fine-tuning improve on the targets that need $S$, and stay where it was on
the targets that already worked? Every answer is the same measurement: for a fixed panel of
\emph{probe} targets, never in any pretraining set, the fine-tuned loss $L(D)$ against the
from-scratch $L(D)$ over the number of training events $D$.

\paragraph{Design.} Chapter one pretrains on one $2\to2$ process ($ee\to u\bar u$) and
fine-tunes a near probe ($ee\to d\bar d$, same topology, other couplings) and a far one
($u\bar u\to gg$: gluons, $t/u$-channel exchange, the gluon self-coupling). The ladder then
adds one kind of structure per rung, each probed by a target that needs it: massless
$s$-channel; electroweak $t$-channel ($ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$, whose $t$-channel is a $W$, while the rung's
Bhabha exchanges a $\gamma$ or $Z$; no rung before $6$ has a $W$); external photons ($ee\to Z\gamma$);
gluon exchange and colour ($ud\to ud$ near, $u\bar u\to gg$ far); masses ($ee\to t\bar t$,
$ee\to W^+W^-$); multiplicity; one loop (tree/loop pairs; $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop, whose
tree is pretrained on, and $ee\to d\bar d$ at one loop, whose tree is not); divergences.
The $t/u$-channel, gluon and photon structures are separate rungs, not one. Multiplicity
is probed on the $Z+ng$ family ($u\bar u\to Zg$, $Zgg$, $Zggg$, the fine-tune targets), with
the $W+ng$ family on the pretraining side, added one multiplicity at a time; pretraining up to
$2\to3$ and fine-tuning $Zggg$ tests extrapolation in multiplicity. Divergences are several
types, not only the deep-IR pools: soft and collinear emission, initial-state collinear
($u\bar u\to Zg$ at small $p_{T,Z}$), the $t$-channel forward peak, the quasi-collinear massive
limit (the dead cone, $ee\to b\bar bg$), narrow resonances, the $t\bar t$ threshold (Coulomb
$1/\beta$ at one loop) and Sudakov logarithms. Every one-loop pool in the study is MadLoop's QCD virtual
(\code{[virt=QCD]}, its finite part at $\mu=\sqrt s$; \code{mg5\_pipeline\_final.py}, \code{tools/nlo\_virtual\_pipeline.py}):
no electroweak loop, hence no electroweak Sudakov logarithm, is in any target. Each enters as a star
arm (the first rung plus one axis, scored on its own probe and on the first rung's) next to the
ladder, which shows what adding more and more structure does. The finale is the model with every
structure against scratch on every held-out probe. The rungs as built (\code{recipes/transfer\_ladder\_r1..9.yaml},
cumulative, $10^5$ training events per tree process): $ee\to u\bar u$, $\mu\mu$, $\nu_\mu\bar\nu_\mu$; $+$Bhabha;
$+ee\to\gamma\gamma$ (the only clean $2\to2$ photon process: $u\bar u\to\gamma\gamma$ is the same dataset);
$+u\bar u\to d\bar d$, $uu\to uu$, $u\bar u\to u\bar u$, $dd\to dd$; $+ee\to b\bar b$, $u\bar u\to t\bar t$, $ee\to ZZ$,
$ee\to ZH$; $+u\bar d\to Wg$; $+Wgg$; $+Wggg$; $+ee\to u\bar u$, $u\bar u\to d\bar d$ and $u\bar u\to ZZ$ at one loop
(trees in earlier rungs; $u\bar u\to ZZ$ is $ee\to ZZ$). No probe and nothing in a probe's equality class
is in any rung.

\paragraph{Protocol.} Every pretraining process has $10^5$ training events (to be revisited if
$2\times$ or $3\times$ that changes a rung) and every pretraining is meant to run to convergence, capped at $64$k steps. The
probes run on $D=10^{k/2}$, $k=2,\dots,10$ ($10$ to $10^5$), prefixes of one shuffled $10^5$
pool, so the smaller sets are nested in the larger; validation and test are fixed $10^4$-event
splits at every $D$ (one-loop pools hold $5\times10^4$ training events, so their grid stops at
$k=9$). Each $(\text{probe},D)$ cell is one single-fidelity DyHPO of $8$ trials
(\code{sweep/gen\_transfer\_pilot.py}), searched wide at first, to be narrowed once
patterns show: the lr window is one decade either side of $\eta^\ast(t)$ of the high-$D$
row (Sec.~\ref{sec:lrlaw}) times $\sqrt{B/16384}$, Adam's square-root batch rule, with the
batch $B=\min(16384,D/2)$, so the batch moves with $D$ (both the user's choice). One seed per
cell, until the noise is known. What depends on the momenta only (their scale, and which
propagator factors the target divides out) is fitted on the whole pool's phase-space points,
which carry no labels (\code{data.phase\_space\_on\_pool}); the amplitude transform and its
mean and standard deviation on the $D$ training events. A fine-tune fits its own target's
amplitude statistics (\code{fine\_tune.target\_stats: own}); it used to inherit the parent's,
which would have standardized $ee\to d\bar d$ with $ee\to u\bar u$'s numbers. Every loss below
is the MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint, $\vlnr\cdot\sigma^2$ with $\sigma$
the run's \code{prepd\_std}, the one unit across cells. Several sweeps side by side are
submitted chained (\code{sweep\_manager submit --chain}): each sweep's three random start-up
trials together, then every guided trial once the one $W$ places back in its sweep has
reported, longest trials first. Before that, with free GPUs every round started at once and the
searches were close to random over their candidate sets.

\paragraph{Datasets that are the same process count once.} Two catalog trees are the same
dataset if one $|\mathcal M|^2$ is a constant multiple of the other at every phase-space point
up to a relabelling of the legs, since per-dataset standardization removes the constant.
\code{tools/equal\_processes.py} evaluates both on $48$ shared points per pair ($2221$ pairs)
and finds $18$ classes (\code{analysis/process\_equality/equal\_processes.json}), with a clean
gap: the equal pairs agree to $2.5\times10^{-15}$, the nearest unequal pair differs by
$2.3\times10^{-5}$. The classes: the massless flavour relabels ($ee\to u\bar u=c\bar c$, also
$=u\bar u\to\mu^+\mu^-$ by crossing; $d\bar d=s\bar s$ with and without a gluon; $ud\to ud=cs\to
cs$; $\nu_\mu=\nu_\tau$; $ud\to ud+\{\gamma,\gamma\gamma,g\gamma\}=us\to us+\dots$),
$ee\to\gamma\gamma=u\bar u\to\gamma\gamma$, and $ee\to X=u\bar u\to X$ for nine colourless
boson final states where the fermion line's couplings factor out ($ZZ$, $Z\gamma$, $ZH$,
$Z\gamma\gamma$, \dots; not with a Higgs and more than one other boson, $\sim10^{-4}$ apart). So a
probe's class is held out whole: with $ee\to Z\gamma$ a probe, $u\bar u\to Z\gamma$ is in no
pretraining set. The $448$-process catalog carries about ten such duplicates.

\paragraph{Horizons: calibrated on $ee\to d\bar d$, applied to every probe.} One fixed-HP run
per $D$ at a long horizon (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_calib}, \code{calib\_ee\_ddbar.json}): up to
$D=316$ the best checkpoint comes early and grows with $D$ ($160$, $640$, $2560$, $4160$ steps of
$8000$); from $10^3$ on it is at the end of the horizon, which with the cosine anneal does not by
itself mean undertrained. The cells that converged early, while the lr was still high, get
$1.1\times$ their best step, so the anneal lands where they had converged ($176$, $704$, $2816$,
$4576$ steps for $k=2$--$5$); $10^3$--$10^4$ keep $8000$ (the user's rule). The same horizons
serve every probe (the user's call, unverified per probe). At large $D$
(Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_long}) $ee\to d\bar d$ keeps improving to $64$k steps
($3.1\times10^{-7}$ at $10^{4.5}$, $1.1\times10^{-7}$ at $10^5$); the $16$k points used lr
$1.6\times10^{-3}$, the $32$k and $64$k ones $3.0\times10^{-3}$, so only $32\to64$k is a clean
horizon step ($1.5\times$ and $22\times$). $64$k steps is the cap for this study. On $u\bar u\to
gg$ (the $t$-channel factor on) $16$k and $32$k steps at the search's best HPs land where $8$k did
($0.8$--$2.3\times10^{-5}$ at $10^{3.5}$ and $10^4$): its flattening was not the horizon, and
the validation curves jump between $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-2}$ until the anneal. The next paragraph
finds the cause.

\paragraph{From $D=10^{3}$ up the curves are at fixed compute.} The $ee\to d\bar d$ horizons do not
carry over to every probe. Four probes re-run at $32$k steps at each cell's best HPs
(Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_horizon}, \code{horizon\_test.py}): at $D=10^{3.5}$ $ud\to ud$, $ee\to t\bar t$
and $ee\to Z\gamma$ drop $4.3$--$4.5\times$, at $10^4$ $ee\to t\bar t$ drops $4.8\times$ and $ee\to
Z\gamma$ $1.2\times$, and $u\bar u\to Zg$ at $10^4$ does not move; the best checkpoints sit at the last
step, and nearly all of the gain comes in the last fifth of the anneal. Two runs diverged, both at the
largest learning rates of the eight (tuned for $8$k steps, where the lr law wants a lower one for a
longer horizon): $ud\to ud$ at $10^4$ at step $21851$ and $u\bar u\to Zg$ at $10^{3.5}$ at step $24794$,
before the anneal; they are drawn at their best validation before diverging. So the flattening of the
scratch curves between $10^{3.5}$ and $10^4$ is mostly the common $8$k-step horizon, not the data. The
study keeps that horizon for $D=10^3$--$10^4$ (the user's call): the curves there are compared at fixed
compute, scratch and fine-tune alike, and longer runs come only after everything else.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/horizon_test_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/horizon_test_b.pdf}}
\caption{The best trial of the $8$k-step search re-run at $32$k steps with the same HPs, (a) $D=10^{3.5}$,
(b) $D=10^4$: MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint; a cross marks a run that diverged,
at its best validation before the divergence.}
\label{fig:ladder_horizon}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/calib_scaling_ee_ddbar.pdf}
\caption{$ee\to d\bar d$ from scratch, one run per $D$ at fixed HPs (the centre of each cell's
lr window) and a long horizon: MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint.}
\label{fig:ladder_calib}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/long_runs_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/long_runs_b.pdf}}
\caption{Loss at the best checkpoint against the horizon at large $D$, one run per point; open
markers the $8$k-step search best of the cell. (a) $ee\to d\bar d$ at fixed HPs, the marker the
run's lr; (b) $u\bar u\to gg$ with the $t$-channel factor on, at the cell's best search HPs for
$10^{3.5}$ and $10^4$ and at the window centre above.}
\label{fig:ladder_long}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The target factors: off.} The $t$-channel factor
(\code{data.target\_propagator\_tchannel}, adopted at the catalog working point,
Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}) set the $\sim10^{-5}$ floor of the $t/u$-channel $2\to2$ probes. At
each cell's search HPs switching it off lowers the loss $11$--$290\times$ in all six cells of
$u\bar u\to gg$, $u\bar u\to Zg$ and $ee\to Z\gamma$ at $D=10^3$ and $10^4$
(\code{tchannel\_ab.json}), and three seeds per arm at $D=10^4$ do not overlap
(Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_ab}b; $u\bar u\to gg$ $4.6\times10^{-6}$--$1.9\times10^{-5}$ on,
$2.0$--$9.3\times10^{-7}$ off; $ee\to Z\gamma$ $1.5$--$3.5\times10^{-5}$ against
$4.5\times10^{-7}$--$1.1\times10^{-6}$). The spread of $\ln|\mathcal M|^2$ over the pool, which
the run log prints before and after the factors, says why: two massless exchanges ($t$ and $u$)
divided out of a target that does not carry the full $1/(tu)$ they assume widen it ($ud\to ud$
$6.4\to34.4$, $u\bar u\to gg$ $3.8\to15.0$, $ee\to Z\gamma$ $4.5\to8.6$, $u\bar u\to Zg$
$4.7\to8.3$). The one probe where it narrows the target is $ee\to W^+W^-$ ($12.9\to5.0$, the
single $\nu$ exchange), and there it helps ($2.8\times$ at $10^3$, $4.8\times$ at $10^4$). The
Breit--Wigner factor widens the target too (it is normalized by $M_Z^4$: $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$
$17.7\to27.6$, $ee\to d\bar d$ $9.1\to13.2$), and with three seeds at each cell's best HPs
(Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_ab}a) switching it off is better in all four cells: the geometric mean
over seeds moves by $0.67$ and $0.44$ for $ee\to d\bar d$ at $10^3$ and $10^4$ (the arms overlap)
and by $0.41$ and $0.08$ for $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ (at $10^4$ they do not). The factors change the
target, so each arm is re-based to MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ with its own $\sigma$. From here on
the study's sweeps run with the $t$-channel factor off (\code{tp2\_}), and after this A/B the
Breit--Wigner factor goes too (\code{tp3\_}, \code{data.target\_propagators} off; the user's call). Its
run logs show it touches four probes only, the ones whose pool reaches the $Z$ pole ($ee\to d\bar d$,
$ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$, $ee\to d\bar d$ and $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop); every other probe carries no
massive factor and keeps its target. The same-HP shortcut of the A/B protocol, each of their $28$
cells re-run once with the factor off at the search's best HPs (\code{bw\_off\_shortcut.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_bwoff}), comes out worse in $23$ cells ($0.07$--$0.9\times$), against the
seeded A/B. The comparison is biased: the factor-on value is the minimum of eight noisy trials and
carries the winner's seed luck, the factor-off value is one draw at those HPs (the next paragraph
measures that bias at $1.5$--$11\times$). So the shortcut cannot settle it here, and the factor-off
arm gets its own search, first on $ee\to d\bar d$ (\code{tp3\_scr\_ee\_ddbar}), extended to the
other three on that result (the user's call). Best against best (\code{bw\_off\_sweep.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_bwoff_sweep}) the factor off costs $1.5$--$2.1\times$ on $ee\to d\bar d$ at $D\le10^2$
and at $10^{3.5}$--$10^4$ ($2.97\times10^{-6}$ against $1.42\times10^{-6}$ at $10^4$) and ties at
$10^{2.5}$--$10^3$; on the other three it swings both ways, from $0.13$ to $9.8\times$ per cell, with
geometric means over the seven cells of $1.48$ ($ee\to d\bar d$), $0.58$ ($ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$), $1.23$
and $0.93$ ($d\bar d$ and $b\bar b$ at one loop), $1.0$ over all $28$ cells. Per probe the factor leans
one way or the other ($ee\to d\bar d$ worse off in five of seven cells, $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ better off by
$1.7\times$), but by no more than two searches' bests differ between seeds (below), and the seeded A/B
at fixed HPs leaned the other way on $ee\to d\bar d$. So the factor buys nothing measurable and stays off. The same search on
$ee\to W^+W^-$ (\code{tp3\_scr}), where only the $t$-channel factor differs, loses in all seven cells
($1.2$--$8.7\times$, geometric mean $2.8\times$): unlike the massless $2\to2$ probes, whose $t$-channel
factor set a floor, the massive $W$ pair is helped by it. Its fine-tunes from the factor-off pretraining
are compared with this factor-off arm, so both share a target.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_sweep_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_sweep_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_sweep_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_sweep_d.pdf}}
\caption{The Breit--Wigner factor on and off, the best of each cell's $8$-trial search (MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint): (a) $ee\to d\bar d$, (b) $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$, (c) $ee\to d\bar d$
at one loop, (d) $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop.}
\label{fig:ladder_bwoff_sweep}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_shortcut_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_shortcut_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_shortcut_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/bw_off_shortcut_d.pdf}}
\caption{The Breit--Wigner factor off, one run per cell at the best HPs of the factor-on search, against
that search's best of eight (MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint): (a) $ee\to d\bar d$,
(b) $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$, (c) $ee\to d\bar d$ at one loop, (d) $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop.}
\label{fig:ladder_bwoff}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{One seed is not a measurement at a cell.} The same A/B gives the seed spread at fixed
HPs: $ee\to d\bar d$ at $D=10^4$ lands between $2.0\times10^{-6}$ and $1.5\times10^{-4}$ over
three seeds ($75\times$) with the factor on and over $45\times$ with it off; a single-run A/B at
default warm-up and $\lambda$ put that cell's factor-on arm at $1.7\times10^{-4}$, $100\times$
the search's best at the same lr. Two independent $8$-trial searches of $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$,
whose target the $t$-channel flag leaves unchanged, differ by up to $5\times$ per cell. Six of the
$36$ seeded runs blew up after their best checkpoint (a jump of five-fold or more between two
validations, as the catalog's $t$-channel seeds did, Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}), five of them in
a factor-off arm: $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ at $10^3$ seeds one and two (step $3680$; the first ends
$23\times$ above its best and never recovers), at $10^4$ seed two (step $3040$, ending $7\times$
above), $ee\to d\bar d$ at $10^4$ seed one in both arms (steps $1760$ off, $3040$ on, ending
$2\times$ above) and $u\bar u\to gg$ seed three off (step $3600$, $1.3\times$). They enter at their
best checkpoint, filled in Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_ab}. So every point of the curves below is one
seed, a $10\times$ wiggle between neighbouring cells can be noise, and the final curves need seeds.
Two more seeds per cell at each cell's best HPs, for the seven probes no open question touches ($98$
runs, \code{seeds\_scratch.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_seeds}), measure both the noise and the selection:
the search best, a minimum over eight trials, sits below the fresh seeds' geometric mean at the same
HPs by a median $1.5\times$ over the $49$ cells, by more than $3\times$ in $10$ of them (up to $23\times$,
$ee\to Z\gamma$ at $10^2$; $11\times$ for $u\bar u\to Zg$ at $10^4$, $2.1\times10^{-7}$ against
$2.3\times10^{-6}$; $u\bar u\to gg$ $4.4\times$ there), and above it in $11$ ($ud\to ud$ at
$10^2$--$10^{3.5}$, $0.26$--$0.88\times$). The noise is the process's: $u\bar u\to Zgg$ and $Zggg$ stay
within $0.9$--$1.9\times$ at every $D$, the $2\to2$ probes swing $3$--$23\times$ in several cells, and
on fresh seeds $u\bar u\to Zg$, $ee\to Z\gamma$
and $ud\to ud$ are flat from $10^3$ to $10^4$ ($1$--$6\times10^{-6}$), the fixed horizon. A fine-tune
against scratch, both a best of eight, carries the same selection on both sides; a single fixed-HP run
against a search best does not, which is what biased the shortcut above.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/seeds_scratch_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/seeds_scratch_b.pdf}}
\caption{Scratch curves with their seed noise: line, geometric mean of two fresh seeds at each cell's
best HPs; band, their range with the search best; open circles, the search best (seed $42$, minimum
of eight trials). (a) $u\bar u\to gg$, $u\bar u\to Zg$, $ee\to Z\gamma$, $ud\to ud$; (b) $u\bar u\to
Zgg$, $u\bar u\to Zggg$, $ee\to t\bar t$.}
\label{fig:ladder_seeds}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/seeded_ab_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/seeded_ab_b.pdf}}
\caption{The target factors on and off, three seeds per arm, each cell at its search's best HPs,
$8000$ steps; the bar is the geometric mean over seeds, filled circles the seeds that blew up
after their best checkpoint. (a) the Breit--Wigner factor, $ee\to d\bar d$ and
$ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ at $D=10^3$ and $10^4$; (b) the $t$-channel factor, $u\bar u\to gg$ and
$ee\to Z\gamma$ at $D=10^4$.}
\label{fig:ladder_ab}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{$ee\to W^+W^-$: the floor is a corner.} With the $t$-channel factor on, $ee\to W^+W^-$
barely moves from $D=10^{3.5}$ to $10^4$ ($2.8\to2.3\times10^{-5}$) while its train loss sits
$8\times$ below test. Its validation error binned in $(\sqrt s,\cos\theta)$ ($\theta$ between the
$e^-$ and the $W^+$; \code{residual\_map.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_resid}) is $10^{-6}$--$10^{-7}$
per bin except in one corner: $\cos\theta\to-1$ at the top of the $\sqrt s$ range, the $W^-$ along
the $e^-$, the $\nu$-exchange forward peak, whose angular width $\sim m_W^2/s$ is about $1\%$ of
the range at $1$\,TeV. The worst $1\%$ of the events carry $99\%$ of the squared error and the two
worst, both at $\cos\theta=-1.000$ and $\sqrt s\approx890$\,GeV, off by $0.5$ in
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$, $86\%$ of it. $ee\to Z\gamma$ (factor off) spreads its error: the worst $1\%$
carry $24\%$. A larger $D$ adds almost no events to that corner, which is why the curve is flat in
$D$. The cause is the factor's floor together with the sampling (\code{ww\_corner.py},
\code{ww\_corner\_ee\_WW.json}): the factor floors $|t|$ at the pool's $10^{-3}$ quantile
($349$\,GeV$^2$, against a kinematic minimum of $69$\,GeV$^2$ set by $m_W$), so below it the target
keeps the full $1/|t|$ spike, and the $11$ validation events there ($0.1\%$) carry $98\%$ of the
squared error; the nine worst all sit below it. The training pool has $100$ such events in $10^5$,
$9$ in the first $10^4$. The mixture sampling cannot fix this by selection: its flat-in-$\log|\mathcal
M|^2$ part keeps what the candidates contain, the $2\to2$ candidates are uniform in $\cos\theta$ and
the adaptive proposal acts on $\sqrt s$ only, and these events already sit at the top $0.04\%$ of the
pool's $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_wwcorner}).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/ww_corner.pdf}
\caption{$ee\to W^+W^-$: training events in the first $D$ of the pool whose $|t|$ lies below the
$t$-channel factor's floor, and within $3\times$ and $10\times$ of it (\code{ww\_corner\_plot.py}).}
\label{fig:ladder_wwcorner}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Steering the $ee\to W^+W^-$ pool once: it pays at large $D$.} The coordinate-free remedy of
Sec.~\ref{sec:div-sampling}, generated once and frozen so scratch and fine-tune share the pool
(\code{tools/steer\_pool.py}; sampling mode \code{steered}, pools tagged \code{\_ssteer}): $2.4$M flat
candidates labelled on CPU, scored by a HETEROSC reference (detached $\sigma$, $\beta=1$, $D=10^5$, $16$k
steps, the mixture pool), $1.2\times10^5$ kept $\propto\sigma^3$ and split into train, val and test; the
$t$-channel normalisation is pinned to the mixture pool's (\code{data.target\_propagator\_tchannel\_norm})
so the target is the baseline's. The reference's $\sigma$ varies little (its $99.9$th percentile is
$3.2\times$ its median), so the selection is mild: the top $0.1\%$ of $\sigma$ make up $1.3\%$ of the
kept events. Each steered run is trained at its cell's baseline-best HPs (the A/B shortcut) and scored
on the mixture test split, the baseline's own, by rebuilding it on that split (\code{cross\_eval.py}).
A first scoring put every steered run $0.12$ too high in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ on every mixture event,
and the mixture-trained reference $0.021$ too low on the steered test. The cause was the rebuild: the
per-event off-shellness input (signed-log $s-M^2$, \code{data.offshell\_per\_event}) is standardized
with a mean and width taken over train, validation and test together, so swapping the test file
moved them and shifted every event's input ($+0.22$ standard deviations for the steered $D=10^4$ run,
$-0.034$ for the reference, the signs of their offsets, and the two models respond alike, $0.54$ and
$0.61$ in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per unit). The pools themselves agree: the same $W$ masses and frame,
and at matched points in $(\sqrt s,\cos\theta)$ the same $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ to $6\times10^{-4}$ on average
(\code{tools/relabel\_check.py} also reproduces the stored values exactly). Within one run the input is
consistent, so no single-pool number changes; the statistics are now recorded in
\code{data\_stats.json} and pinned on a rebuild (\code{data.offshell\_stats}). The same rebuild scored
the candidates when the pool was built, so its $\sigma$ carried a shift of about $-0.03$ standard
deviations: the pool is steered by a slightly perturbed $\sigma$, not an unsteered one. Scored with the
statistics pinned (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_steer}), the steered pool wins where the mixture pool
plateaus, $12.6\times$ at $D=10^4$ ($4.9\times10^{-6}$ against $6.2\times10^{-5}$) and $1.5\times$ at
$10^{3.5}$, at the baseline's HPs, a comparison biased against it, so those two cells are settled. Below,
at the baseline's HPs it lost; with its own $8$-trial search (\code{tps\_scr}) it wins at $10$
($1.3\times$), $10^{2.5}$ ($1.9\times$) and $10^3$ ($1.7\times$) and still loses at $10^{1.5}$ and $10^2$
($6.4$ and $8.7\times$), where selecting $\propto\sigma^3$ leaves a few hundred events too few in the
bulk. So one steered pool helps from a few hundred events up, most where the mixture pool flattens. The study's
$ee\to W^+W^-$ cells therefore take the mixture pool up to $D=10^2$ and the steered pool from $10^{2.5}$ on (the
user's call; the $1.3\times$ at $D=10$ is inside the search noise), scratch and fine-tune alike. With the study's
target (both factors off) on the steered pool, fine-tuning from $ee\to u\bar u$ gains $1.7$, $0.30$, $3.7$ and $0.48\times$
at $D=10^{2.5}$--$10^4$, $0.93$ over the grid of Table~\ref{tab:ladder_transfer_all}, as on the mixture pool: the
steered pool lowers both arms, not their ratio.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/steer_ab.pdf}
\caption{$ee\to W^+W^-$ trained on the mixture pool (each cell's best of $8$ trials) and on the
$\sigma$-steered pool (its own search's best; open, one run at the mixture pool's best HPs), both scored on the mixture test split with the run's own
input statistics (MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint).}
\label{fig:ladder_steer}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/residual_map_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/residual_map_b.pdf}}
\caption{Validation MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per $(\sqrt s,\cos\theta)$ bin at $D=10^4$, the
cell's best trial retrained with its predictions saved: (a) $ee\to W^+W^-$, $t$-channel factor
on; (b) $ee\to Z\gamma$, factor off.}
\label{fig:ladder_resid}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The scratch curves.} Table~\ref{tab:ladder_scratch} and Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_scratch}
give the best trial of every cell up to $D=10^4$, with the $t$-channel factor off wherever it
applies (the $2\to2$ probes with a massless $t/u$-channel exchange; $ee\to W^+W^-$ is shown with
it on, pending the corner) and the Breit--Wigner factor on; the fine-tunes below are compared with the
factor-off searches of the five probes the factors touch, not with these rows. Multiplicity costs the most data: at
$D=10^4$ $u\bar u\to Zg$ is at $2.1\times10^{-7}$, $Zgg$ $1600\times$ above it and $Zggg$ another
$13\times$ above that, both still falling steadily. The $2\to2$ trees reach
$10^{-7}$--$10^{-6}$ by $10^4$, except $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ ($1.2\times10^{-4}$, the widest
target of the panel with the Breit--Wigner factor, the probe where switching it off gained
$12\times$) and $ee\to W^+W^-$. The one-loop probes fall steeply late ($ee\to d\bar d$ at one loop
$30\times$ from $10^3$ to $10^{3.5}$). Before the $t$-channel factor was switched off, the four
$t/u$-channel $2\to2$ probes all flattened near $10^{-5}$ from $D\approx300$: that was the target,
not the data.

$ee\to t\bar t$ & $5.5\text{e-}3$ & $2.8\text{e-}4$ & $2.2\text{e-}5$ & $3.1\text{e-}6$ & $2.6\text{e-}7$ & $2.2\text{e-}7$ & $1.3\text{e-}7$ \\
$ee\to W^+W^{-\ast}$ & $3.7\text{e-}2$ & $2.9\text{e-}3$ & $6.4\text{e-}4$ & $3.5\text{e-}4$ & $2.3\text{e-}4$ & $2.8\text{e-}5$ & $2.3\text{e-}5$ \\
$ee\to d\bar d$ (1-loop) & $4.2$ & $0.13$ & $2.2\text{e-}2$ & $8.6\text{e-}3$ & $1.3\text{e-}3$ & $4.1\text{e-}5$ & $2.0\text{e-}5$ \\

\paragraph{The large-$D$ end, at fixed compute.} One $64$k-step run per probe at $D=10^{4.5}$ and $10^5$
(fixed HPs, lr $1.2\times10^{-3}$, the $t$-channel factor off; Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_large},
\code{large\_d.py}): the $t/u$-channel $2\to2$ probes reach $3$--$12\times10^{-9}$ ($u\bar u\to Zg$
$3.2\times10^{-9}$ at $10^5$), with no sign of the old $10^{-5}$ floor, and most best checkpoints sit at the
last step. Their curve through $D\le10^3$, extended, would put $10^5$ near $10^{-11}$, so at large $D$ they
are limited by steps, at a compute that is not worth paying now. The step from $10^4$ to $10^{4.5}$ also
crosses from $8$k to $64$k steps, so it is no data slope: the $t/u$-channel probes gain $8$--$46\times$
there, $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ $36\times$, the one-loop probes $230$ and $460\times$. At equal compute, $10^{4.5}\to10^5$,
$ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ still falls $8\times$, against $\le2\times$ for the $t/u$-channel probes ($ee\to Z\gamma$
rises), so of the probes measured there the $Z$-pole one is where transfer can still show at large $D$; the
one-loop grid stops at $10^{4.5}$. The eight runs on
Jean Zay ($ud\to ud$, $ee\to t\bar t$, $u\bar u\to Zgg$, $u\bar u\to Zggg$) first ran into the $3$\,h limit at
$23$--$31$k steps; rerun on lxplus to the end (2026-10-03), they give $1.6\times10^{-7}$ and $2.0\times10^{-8}$
($ud\to ud$ at $10^{4.5}$, $10^5$), $1.9$ and $3.2\times10^{-9}$ ($ee\to t\bar t$), $3.7\times10^{-5}$ and $5.8\times10^{-6}$
($u\bar u\to Zgg$), $6.2$ and $2.0\times10^{-4}$ ($u\bar u\to Zggg$), on the grid's target (\code{large\_d\_64k.json}). At
equal compute $ud\to ud$ falls $8\times$ from $10^{4.5}$ to $10^5$, $u\bar u\to Zgg$ $6\times$, $u\bar u\to Zggg$ $3\times$, and
$ee\to t\bar t$ rises $1.7\times$, one run each. The four probes the Breit--Wigner factor touched ran here with it on, so
they were rerun on the grid's target (both factors off; Jean Zay, 2026-10-04): $ee\to d\bar d$ $1.9\times10^{-7}$ at both
$10^{4.5}$ and $10^5$ (limited by steps), $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ $1.2\times10^{-5}$ and $5.8\times10^{-7}$ ($21\times$),
$ee\to d\bar d$ and $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop $8.7\times10^{-7}$ and $1.5\times10^{-6}$ at $10^{4.5}$. The figure keeps the
factor-on points of the first runs.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/large_d_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/large_d_b.pdf}}
\caption{Scratch curves to $D=10^4$ (best of the $8$k-step search) with one $64$k-step run at $10^{4.5}$
and $10^5$ (squares): (a) $u\bar u\to gg$, $u\bar u\to Zg$, $ee\to Z\gamma$; (b) $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$, $ee\to d\bar d$ and
$ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop, all three with the Breit--Wigner factor on. The two sides are at different
compute.}
\label{fig:ladder_large}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Chapter one: the near probe gains at every $D$, the far one once its lr is free.} The $ee\to u\bar u$
pretraining (one DyHPO, $8$ trials, $32$k steps, $10^5$ events; best trial lr
$6.7\times10^{-4}$, $\vlnr=1.6\times10^{-8}$, $2.6\times10^{-7}$ in MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$,
best checkpoint at the last step, so not converged) is fine-tuned on both chapter-one probes, the same cells and
horizons as scratch, the full model retrained with layer decay (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_ch1}).
$ee\to d\bar d$ gains at every $D$: $5$--$6\times$ at $10$--$10^{1.5}$, $16$--$19\times$ at
$10^2$--$10^{2.5}$, $5\times$ at $10^3$ and $2\times$ at $10^{3.5}$--$10^4$ ($6.4\times10^{-7}$
against $1.4\times10^{-6}$). $u\bar u\to gg$ (both arms with the $t$-channel factor off) loses
$5$--$8\times$ at every $D$ up to $10^{2.5}$ and $1.8\times$ at $10^3$, and gains only at
$10^{3.5}$ ($3.5\times$) and $10^4$ ($1.3\times$, $7.6\times10^{-8}$). Four consecutive cells
losing by a similar factor is not the seed noise above, but whether it is transfer or the
fine-tune's search (its lr is fixed at the pretraining's times $\sqrt{B/16384}$, and only
\code{lr\_scale}$\in[0.1,10]$ and the layer decay move it) is not yet separated: every cell's best
\code{lr\_scale}, both probes, sits in the top decade ($3$--$9$), the window's ceiling. The expected
ordering, gain falling with distance, holds; that it goes negative at small $D$ was not expected. With
the Breit--Wigner factor off the pretraining is re-run once at the best trial's HPs
(\code{tp3\_pre\_ee\_uu}; it stopped at step $30080$ of $32000$ on a time limit, its best checkpoint there,
$94\%$ through the anneal, at $1.18\times10^{-7}$, half the factor-on pretraining's), and both chapter-one
probes are fine-tuned from it again with \code{lr\_scale} searched over $[1,100]$ (\code{tp3\_ft};
Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_ch1}, against the factor-off scratch arms). $ee\to d\bar d$ (keeping the pretraining's
off-shellness scale, next paragraph) gains at every $D$, $1.8\times$ at $10^3$ and $3.9$--$19\times$ elsewhere, $4.0\times$ at $10^4$
($7.4\times10^{-7}$ against $3.0\times10^{-6}$); its best \code{lr\_scale} is $1$--$10$ in every cell but one ($80$ at
$10^{1.5}$). $u\bar u\to gg$ now loses only at $10^{2.5}$ ($2.2\times$), gains $1.6$--$4.1\times$ at the other $D$ up
to $10^{3.5}$ and ties at $10^4$ ($1.4\times10^{-7}$ against $1.0\times10^{-7}$); its best \code{lr\_scale} is $57$ or
more in six of seven cells, up to $930$ once the window was extended (the next paragraph's cell rule). So the old loss was mostly the capped lr: the far probe wants a fine-tuning lr close
to scratch's, $\sqrt{B/16384}$ having shrunk the pretraining's by up to $57\times$ at small $D$. The two
values $57$ and $80$ are two candidate points, recurring across cells because every sweep draws the
same candidates (hand-off).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/transfer_ch1_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/transfer_ch1_b.pdf}}
\caption{Chapter one with both target factors off: best trial per cell from scratch and fine-tuned
from the factor-off $ee\to u\bar u$ pretraining, MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ at the best checkpoint;
open marker, a cell with fewer than $7$ of $8$ trials in. (a) $ee\to d\bar d$; (b) $u\bar u\to gg$ (no
factor reaches its pool, so its scratch arm is the $t$-channel-off search).}
\label{fig:ladder_ch1}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Every probe from $ee\to u\bar u$: the near probe, $ud\to ud$ and the one-loop probes gain.}
All twelve probes fine-tuned from the same factor-off pretraining, the same cells, horizons and search (\code{lr\_scale}
over $[1,100]$), each against the scratch search on the same target (the one with equal \code{prepd\_std} in every cell:
\code{tp3\_scr} for the four $Z$-window probes and $ee\to W^+W^-$, \code{tp2\_scr} for $ee\to Z\gamma$, $ud\to ud$,
$u\bar u\to gg$ and $Zg$, \code{tp\_scr} for $ee\to t\bar t$, $Zgg$ and $Zggg$, which no factor reaches;
$ee\to W^+W^-$ on the steered pool from $D=10^{2.5}$, scored on its own validation split). The fine-tune keeps the
pretraining's scale on the off-shellness input (\code{tp3\_ftp}, the seven probes with an internal $Z$; the other five
see only columns the pretraining saw constant, \code{tp3\_ft}). Refitting that input on the probe instead, as a first
round did, costs those seven a geometric mean over $D$ of $1.3$--$2.1\times$ where the probe's off-shellness differs
from $ee\to u\bar u$'s ($ee\to d\bar d$ at one loop $2.1$, $\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ $1.7$, $b\bar b$ at one loop $1.6$,
$W^+W^-$ $1.4$, $ud\to ud$ $1.3$) and nothing on $ee\to d\bar d$ ($0.98$), whose pool spans the same $\sqrt s$ window
as the pretraining's, and $ee\to t\bar t$ ($0.96$). Each cell is one search: where its best sat in the top $15\%$ of
the lr window ($31$ scratch cells, $46$ fine-tune cells, both arms alike) the search was extended in place, its range
widened and trials added until the best was inside (\code{sweep/extend\_sweep.py}), so a cell holds $8$ to $24$
trials; its value is that search's best (\code{analysis/transfer/cells.py}). The gain
$L_\text{scratch}/L_\text{fine-tune}$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_transfer_all}, Table~\ref{tab:ladder_transfer_all}; the
losses in Figs.~\ref{fig:ladder_transfer_loss_ee} and~\ref{fig:ladder_transfer_loss_qcd}; \code{transfer\_all.py}):
$ee\to d\bar d$ gains in every cell ($1.8$--$19\times$, geometric mean over $D$ $5.3$); $ud\to ud$ $5.5$--$16\times$
from $10^{2.5}$ on, growing with $D$, and loses up to $3\times$ below (mean $3.7$); $ee\to d\bar d$ and $b\bar b$ at one
loop gain in every cell ($1.4$--$9.3\times$, means $3.3$ and $2.7$). The other eight have means of $0.93$--$1.7$, with
cells from $0.27$ to $8\times$ in both directions; two searches' bests differ by up to $5\times$ between seeds, so a
cell alone does not separate transfer from noise. Extending the lr windows moved $ee\to\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ most: its
scratch searches had been held low, and its mean fell from $2.6$ to $1.0$. The massive $s$-channel probe,
$ee\to t\bar t$ (the pretraining's topology with a mass, mean $1.7$), the $Z+ng$ family and $ee\to Z\gamma$ gain nothing
clear. Where the gain is clear the search keeps the fine-tune lr low (best \code{lr\_scale} $1$--$15$ in most cells of
$ee\to d\bar d$ and both one-loop probes); elsewhere it is mostly $57$ or more, near scratch's lr, so for those probes
the pretraining is little more than an initialisation the search trains away from. $ud\to ud$ gains at large $D$ with
\code{lr\_scale} $40$--$80$ all the same.

\paragraph{The ladder's pretraining: not converged at $64$k steps, one optimum on every rung, and the one-loop rung costs every process $\sim10\times$.}
One single-fidelity search per rung ($64$k steps, both target factors off, the lr window one decade either
side of $\eta^\ast(64\text{k})=1.2\times10^{-3}$; \code{tp3\_ladder\_r}$n$, \code{ladder\_pretrain.py}). Rungs $1$--$4$
(Jean Zay) ran all $8$ trials. Rungs $5$--$9$ (lxplus) were cut short (2026-10-02, the user's call): with their first
three to five trials in, every rung's optimum sat in the same region, and nine of the ten guided trials then running
repeated points measured $7$--$1200\times$ worse or divergent on other rungs. Those were cancelled; rung $6$'s one new
point (hp1, lr $2.7\times10^{-3}$, $\lambda=5\times10^{-10}$) ran on and came out $12\times$ above its rung's best, and
each of rungs $5$--$9$ ran the two points that won the second wave on rungs $1$--$2$ (hp47, lr $1.1\times10^{-3}$, EMA
on; hp73, lr $1.8\times10^{-3}$, EMA off; \code{generate\_sweep.py --extend --fixed-hp}). One of the two is the best on
every one of rungs $5$--$9$, $1.2$, $1.5$, $6.3$, $2.0$ and $1.6\times$ below the first wave's. The bests, rungs
$1$--$9$: $3.6$, $2.9$, $3.5$, $5.8$, $9.1$, $8.5$, $4.9$, $15\times10^{-8}$ and $1.3\times10^{-6}$ (hp47, hp73, hp185,
hp15, hp47, hp73, hp73, hp47, hp73). Rung $7$ below rungs $5$ and $6$ is within what $5$--$7$ trials per rung resolve.
A trial takes $6.3$\,h.
Every finished curve still falls when the anneal ends: the best checkpoint sits at $58$--$64$k steps
(Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_pre_curves}). The optimum is the same region on every rung (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_pre_hpo}): lr
$0.8$--$2\times10^{-3}$ with $\lambda\le3\times10^{-9}$; $\lambda$ near $10^{-7}$--$10^{-6}$ costs $10$--$1000\times$,
and six trials diverged (NaN gradients, at $36$--$60$k steps), all with $\lambda\ge3\times10^{-7}$ (lr $0.8$--$4.7\times10^{-3}$); their best checkpoints before the blow-up, rescored on the validation split (\code{tools/eval\_best\_val.py}; their logs print the loss with the $L_2$ term, which swamps it), sit $8$--$2200\times$ above their rung's best, so none changes a rung's best. Every sweep starts from the same three random candidates, so the first wave tested only three points
per rung; the guided trials improved the best by $2.0$, $2.1$ and $1.1\times$ on rungs $1$--$3$ and not on rung $4$. At
the rung's best checkpoint (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_pre_dataset}) each process's MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ moves within
$\sim3\times$ from rung to rung up to rung $8$ and jumps $\sim10\times$ at rung $9$, where the one-loop sets come in:
$ee\to u\bar u$ $1.1\times10^{-7}$ at rung $1$, $1.7$--$3.6\times10^{-7}$ at rungs $4$--$8$, $2.6\times10^{-6}$ at rung
$9$; the four-quark processes $9$--$12\times$ between rungs $4$ and $9$. At a fixed $64$k steps every added process takes
its share of the same budget, and the one-loop targets take more of it.

\paragraph{The pretraining protocol: hp73 alone, from 2026-10-04.}
Every pretraining search draws from the same candidate pool, so an hp index is the same point on all sixteen $64$k-step
pretrainings (rungs $1$--$9$, the $ee\to u\bar u$ pretraining, the six star arms; \code{pretrain\_hp.py}, collected on
every site, \code{pretrain\_hp.json}). The good region is the same on all of them (Fig.~\ref{fig:pretrain_hp}): lr
$0.8$--$2\times10^{-3}$ with $\lambda\le3\times10^{-9}$, $10$--$10^4\times$ worse outside it, and the four points that
recur across the searches (hp15, hp47, hp73, hp95) all sit in it. hp73 (lr $1.8\times10^{-3}$, $\lambda=1.4\times10^{-10}$,
warm-up $0.08$, EMA off) ran on $13$: the best trial on seven (rungs $2$, $6$, $7$, $9$; the soft, dead-cone and threshold
arms, the last from the rescored best checkpoint of a run whose result write hit the EOS quota), within $1.02$--$1.37\times$ of
the best on five more (rungs $5$, $8$; the ISR, resonance and Sudakov arms), and $2.3\times$ above it on the
$ee\to u\bar u$ pretraining, where hp95 is the best. hp47 is
$4.9\times$ above on rung $7$ and $4.1\times$ on the threshold arm, hp95 $9$--$13\times$ on rungs $7$ and $8$. Every
best is the best of $4$--$8$ trials, so the ratios rank the points, not the optimum. From 2026-10-04 a new pretraining runs
hp73 alone (the user's call; until then hp47 and hp73).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/pretrain_hp_rank.pdf}\\[1ex]
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/pretrain_hp_landscape.pdf}
\caption{Every $64$k-step pretraining trial's best $\vlnr$ over its pretraining's best. Top: per pretraining, the
four recurring points marked (open: the best checkpoint rescored, the run diverged or could not write its result).
Bottom: against the searched HPs (open: EMA off). The six diverged trials are drawn at their rescored best checkpoint, $30$--$230\times$ above their pretraining's best
(hp30 on the $ee\to u\bar u$ pretraining and the soft, ISR and resonance arms, hp18 on the dead-cone and Sudakov arms;
all with $\lambda\ge3\times10^{-7}$). Not drawn: the $22$ stopped trials (cancelled or lost; \code{pretrain\_hp.py} lists them).}
\label{fig:pretrain_hp}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The fine-tune protocol's landscape: no HP matters much at small $D$, a low lr at large $D$, and the guided
trials no better than random ones.} Every cell of the fine-tune grids ($1043$ cells, a cell one sweep's first five trials:
rungs $1$--$9$,
$ee\to u\bar u$ at $32$k and $64$k, the star arms; \code{finetune\_hp.py}, \code{finetune\_hp.json}, collected on every
site), each trial over its cell's best (Fig.~\ref{fig:finetune_hp}). Up to $D=10^2$ the cell is flat: the median trial is
$1.2$--$1.3\times$ above the best, nothing in lr, $\lambda$, warm-up or EMA moves it, and the best checkpoint comes at
$20$--$33\%$ of the horizon. From $D=10^{2.5}$ on the spread grows ($5$--$8\times$ for the worst of five at $D\ge10^3$)
and one knob shows: lr above $\sim3\times10^{-3}$ costs $1.4$--$4.3\times$ at $D\ge10^3$, the best lr sits at
$1$--$2.5\times10^{-3}$ (median $1.9\times10^{-3}$ at $D=10^{3.5}$, $10^4$; $23\%$ of the bests below $1.3\times10^{-3}$, near
the window's floor); $\lambda$ near $10^{-6}$ costs $1.9$--$3.8\times$ there; warm-up and EMA do nothing at any $D$. In the $915$ cells that ran the protocol as
planned (three random start-up trials, then two guided; in the others failed start-up trials had been replaced) the two
DyHPO-guided trials hold the cell's best in $28$--$46\%$ of the cells, about what two of five random draws would ($40\%$);
the best of the three start-up trials is the cell's best in the median cell at every $D$, $1.2$--$2.0\times$ above it at
the 90th percentile. A trial in the good region (lr $\le2.5\times10^{-3}$, $\lambda\le10^{-7}$) is not safe alone: $1.1$--$1.3\times$
above the cell's best in the median, $3.3$--$4.5\times$ at the 90th percentile for $D\ge10^2$, so the scatter between trials
of a cell is wider than any HP trend, and a fixed point (the pretraining's answer) would not do here. At large $D$ the
best checkpoint sits at the end of the horizon ($94$--$98\%$ for $D\ge10^3$): no steps to save there; the cells up to
$D=10^2$, where they could be cut, cost $41$ of the grid's $421$ GPU-h.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_d.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_e.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_landscape_f.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/finetune_hp_protocol.pdf}
\caption{The fine-tune grids' trials (the searched space: the fine-tune lr, $\lambda$, warm-up, $\eta_{\min}$, EMA and its
decay; \code{lr\_scale} and \code{layer\_decay} fixed at $1$), each over its cell's best, per $D$. (a)--(e): the median
against lr, $\lambda$, warm-up, $\eta_{\min}$ and the EMA decay (EMA-on trials), in bins with ten trials or more; (f) with EMA
off and on. Bars: $16$th--$84$th percentile over $200$ resamples of the cells. Bottom left: the best of the first $n$ trials (evaluation
order: three random start-up trials, then two guided) over the best of five, median (solid) and 90th percentile
(dotted). Bottom right: where the cell's best trial has its best checkpoint, and its first validation within $1.1\times$
of it, as a fraction of the horizon (median; bars: the 10th--90th percentile over cells, a spread, not an uncertainty).
A cell is one sweep's first five trials in evaluation order; a cell run on two sites (started on one, generated again
on the other by a move: rung $9$ on five probes at $D\ge10^3$, the soft arm on $ee\to d\bar dg$ at $10^{3.5}$, $10^4$)
keeps the site with more results. The bottom-left panel uses the $915$ cells that ran three start-up then two guided
trials. Not used, fewer than five results in one sweep: rung $9$'s split cells but the soft arm's, rung $1$ on
$u\bar d\to e^+\nu_e$ at $D\ge10^3$, rung $4$ on the two one-loop probes at $D=10^2$ and on $ee\to t\bar t$ at
$10^{2.5}$, $ee\to u\bar u$ on $Z\gamma$, $d\bar d$ at one loop and $gg$ at $10^{2.5}$ (\code{finetune\_hp.py} lists
them).}
\label{fig:finetune_hp}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The ladder, preliminary: gluon exchange and multiplicity transfer from the rungs that carry them.} Each
probe fine-tuned at $D=10$, $10^2$, $10^3$, $10^4$ from ladder rungs $4$ (up to gluon exchange) and $9$ (every rung,
one loop included), from the best pretraining trial of each rung's first wave (lr $9.3\times10^{-4}$, EMA on; still rung $4$'s best
after all eight trials, rung $9$'s with three in;
\code{tp3\_r4ftp}, \code{tp3\_r9ftp}, single $8$-trial searches, not extended at the lr window's edge), against the same
scratch cells (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_prelim}, \code{ladder\_prelim.py}; $ee\to W^+W^-$ on the mixture pool here). Where
a rung adds the structure a probe needs, its gain rises: $u\bar u\to gg$ from $1.8$ ($ee\to u\bar u$) to $4.7$ and $4.3$,
$u\bar u\to Zg$ to $4.0$ and $Zggg$ to $2.8$ once the $W+ng$ family is in (rung $9$; rung $4$ leaves them at $1.1$ and
$1.3$), $ud\to ud$ to $5.9$ from rung $9$ ($52\times$ at $10^4$), and $ee\to b\bar b$ at one loop to $4.5$ and $6.9$.
The near probe loses a little ($ee\to d\bar d$ $4.3\to2.9$, $2.7$): every pretraining process gets a smaller share of the
same $64$k steps as the rungs grow, though each one's own loss moves within $\sim3\times$ up to rung $8$ and jumps only
at rung $9$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_pre_dataset}, the pretraining paragraph above). $ee\to Z\gamma$ and $ee\to W^+W^-$ stay near $1$ from every pretraining. Four
cells per probe and one search each: a direction, not a measurement.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/ladder_prelim.pdf}
\caption{Fine-tuned against scratch, $L_\text{scratch}/L_\text{fine-tune}$, from the $ee\to u\bar u$ pretraining and from
ladder rungs $4$ and $9$: the geometric mean over $D=10,10^2,10^3,10^4$, the bar the range of the four cells. (a) the
$ee$ probes, (b) the photon and QCD probes; $^{(1)}$ one loop.}
\label{fig:ladder_prelim}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The fine-tune wants a fixed lr, not a scaled one.} Over the $84$ cells fine-tuned from $ee\to u\bar u$
(Table~\ref{tab:ladder_transfer_all}), the best trial's effective lr ($\code{training.lr}\times\code{lr\_scale}$) sits
at a median $2.3\times10^{-3}$, half the cells within $0.8$--$7\times10^{-3}$, with no trend in $D$; the
$\sqrt{B/16384}$ scaling of \code{training.lr} ($1.2\times10^{-5}$ at $D=10$) is what pushed the best \code{lr\_scale} to
$10$--$900$. A window of $[10^{-3},10^{-2}]$ holds a trial in $80$ of the $84$ cells and costs the median cell nothing
($2.5\times$ at the $90$th percentile). \code{layer\_decay} shows no effect (median correlation with the loss $+0.08$,
the bests spread over the whole $[0.75,1]$). The scratch optimum is $\sim3\times$ lower (median $9\times10^{-4}$). The
fine-tune grid from the rungs therefore searches \code{training.lr} over $[10^{-3},10^{-2}]$ with \code{lr\_scale} and
\code{layer\_decay} fixed at $1$, $5$ trials per cell, a candidate seed per sweep (\code{gen\_transfer\_pilot.py --eff-lr};
the user's call, 2026-10-02).

\paragraph{The fine-tune grid: the rungs that add multiplicity gain most, and the $ee\to u\bar u$ budget does not
matter.} Each of the twelve probes fine-tuned from every rung's final best and from $ee\to u\bar u$ ($32$k and $64$k
parents) in that setup (\code{tp3\_r}$n$\code{fte}, \code{tp3\_uufte}, \code{tp3\_uu64fte}; Fig.~\ref{fig:rung_grid},
\code{rung\_grid.py}), collected from all three sites on 2026-10-04 at 12:00: every one of the $11\times84$ cells has a
value, $412$--$421$ trials per pretraining (the last $16$, in rung $4$ and \code{tp3\_uufte}, were lost to the EOS quota and
are being replaced). The gain over scratch, a geometric mean over the $84$ cells, is $2.1$, $2.2$, $2.1$, $2.3$, $2.2$,
$2.4$ from rungs $1$--$6$, $3.3$, $3.2$, $3.4$ from rungs $7$--$9$ (the first rung with $2\to3$), and $1.7$ from $ee\to u\bar u$
at either budget ($1.69$ from $32$k, $1.70$ from $64$k steps), so rung $1$'s lead over $ee\to u\bar u$ is the two other
processes, not the steps. Summed over the probes the gain is larger at $D\ge10^{2.5}$ than at $D\le100$ for every
pretraining (rungs $7$--$9$: $3.7$--$4.2\times$ against $2.5\times$); the probes that gain most at small $D$ are single
cases, below. $u\bar u\to Zggg$ is $1.7$--$3.1\times$ lower from rung $8$ ($+2\to4$) than from rung $7$ at every $D$.

fine-tuned from $ee\to u\bar u$ ($32$k and $64$k parents) and from every ladder rung in the grid's setup (solid), and the

\paragraph{Each probe against the structure it needs.} Fig.~\ref{fig:rung_focus} (\code{rung\_focus.py}) draws, per
probe, scratch, the first rung that adds the probe's structure, the rung below it (the same pretraining without it; $ee\to
u\bar u$ below rung $1$), and the pretraining that does best overall, every other one grey. Best overall is the largest mean
of $\log_{10}(L_{\rm scratch}/L_{\rm fine\text{-}tune})$ over the probe's cells, each half-decade of $D$ weighted the same (the
user asked for a rule, 2026-10-02, weighing large $D$, where a factor in data matters most, against small $D$, noisier but
what rare processes need; weighting large $D$ or small $D$ more moves the leader only where the top two are within
$1.13\times$); leaders within $1.2\times$ of the best are reported as tied, $1.2$ being a provisional bound on what one seed's
five-trial search resolves, to be replaced by the seed spread. The rung with the structure is below the one without it
at every $D$ only for multiplicity: $u\bar u\to Zggg$ (rung $8$, $1.7$--$3.1\times$) and $u\bar u\to Zgg$ (rung $7$,
$1.1$--$1.8\times$); $ud\to ud$ gains from rung $4$ (gluon exchange) only at $D\le10^2$ ($2$--$7\times$). On the other
probes the ratio swings both ways cell to cell ($ee\to t\bar t$ from rung $5$: $7\times$ lower at $D=10$, $4\times$
higher at $10^3$), the scale of one seed's spread at a cell (Fig.~\ref{fig:ladder_seeds}); Table~\ref{tab:rung_focus_ratio}
gives the ratio at every probe and $D$.
